Analysis of a central MUSCL-type scheme for conservation laws with discontinuous flux
Nikhil Manoj, Sudarshan Kumar K.

Abstract
In this article, we propose a second-order central scheme of the Nessyahu-Tadmor-type for a class of scalar conservation laws with discontinuous flux and present its convergence analysis. Since solutions to problems with discontinuous flux generally do not belong to the space of bounded variation (BV), we employ the theory of compensated compactness to establish the convergence of approximate solutions. A major component of our analysis involves deriving the maximum principle and showing the W loc − 1 , 2 \mathrm{W}^{-1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}} compactness of a sequence constructed from approximate solutions. The latter is achieved through the derivation of several essential estimates on the approximate solutions. Furthermore, by incorporating a mesh-dependent correction term in the slope limiter, we show that the numerical solutions generated by the proposed second-order scheme converge to the entropy solution. Finally, we validate our theoretical results by presenting numerical examples.



keywords

Hyperbolic conservation laws, Discontinuous flux, Second-order scheme, Compensated compactness, Entropy solution

†
†
runningheads:
Conservation Laws with Discontinuous Flux / NIKHIL MANOJ AND SUDARSHAN KUMAR K.

MSC

35L65, 76A30, 65M08, 65M12

1
Introduction

We are interested in the second-order discretization of the Cauchy problem for scalar conservation laws with spatially varying flux:

\displaystyle u_{t}+f(k(x),u)_{x}

\displaystyle=0\quad\mbox{for}\,\,(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+},

(1.1)

\displaystyle u(x,0)

\displaystyle=u_{0}(x),\quad\mbox{for}\,\,x\in\mathbb{R},

where
t
and
x
are the time and space variables, respectively and
u=u(x,t)
is the unknown quantity. Here, the coefficient
k(x)
in the flux function
f
is allowed to be a discontinuous function of the spatial variable
x
.

1.1
A brief review of conservation laws with discontinuous flux

Conservation laws of the form (
1.1
) with discontinuous coefficient
k(x)
appear in various physical models, including two-phase flow in porous media arising in oil reservoirs
[
30
,
33
]
, sedimentation applications
[
21
,
23
]
and traffic flow with varying road surface conditions
[
18
,
32
,
48
]
, among others. These equations have been the focus of extensive theoretical and numerical studies over the past few decades, for a detailed review see
[
19
,
46
]
and references therein.
A classic example often examined in this context is the two-flux case, which emerges by specifying the flux
f
in (
1.1
) as follows:

\displaystyle f(H(x),u(x,t))

\displaystyle=H(x)f_{r}(u)+(1-H(x))f_{l}(u)=\begin{cases}f_{l}(u)\quad\mbox{for}\,x<0,\\
f_{r}(u)\quad\mbox{for}\,x\geq 0,\end{cases}

(1.2)

where
H
is the Heaviside function.
Another common example involves the flux function having a multiplicative dependency on a discontinuous coefficient, as given by:

\displaystyle f(k(x),u)=k(x)g(u),

(1.3)

for some appropriate function
g.

In this work, we focus on equation (
1.1
) with a general flux function
f(k(x),u)
that does not necessarily have to conform to the cases mentioned above.

It is important to note that standard theoretical tools and numerical methods are not applicable to (
1.1
) when the coefficient
k
is discontinuous. This situation necessitates the development of novel theoretical approaches and specialized numerical methods. This topic has been extensively explored in the literature, where well-posedness is primarily established through suitable numerical approximations; see, for example,
[
5
,
17
,
36
,
37
,
40
]
. For a comprehensive framework on the well-posedness of (
1.1
), we refer to
[
10
]
.
A fundamental aspect in this context is the uniqueness of solutions to these problems, where an entropy condition (or admissibility condition) plays a crucial role in ensuring uniqueness by identifying physically relevant solutions. Several attempts have been made in the literature to distill the physically relevant solution. In particular, the authors in
[
5
]
considered the two-flux case described in (
1.2
) and introduced an infinite family of entropy conditions, referred to as the (A, B) entropy conditions. Each condition within this family ensures uniqueness, giving rise to the concept of (A, B) entropy solutions. This has led to the fact that there is no unique physically relevant solution for scalar conservation laws with discontinuous flux. Instead, the physics of the problems determines the underlying unique solution, which corresponds to a particular (A, B) connection.
In this work, we focus on an entropy condition given by a particular (A, B) connection for each problem, which is typically expressed through a Kruzkov-type entropy condition or adapted entropy formulation, mainly in the framework of
[
21
,
22
,
37
,
38
]
. Such solutions can be identified as the unique vanishing viscosity solution, a concept extensively studied in numerous works
[
11
,
13
,
23
,
36
,
51
,
55
,
60
]
and remains relevant across various models that incorporate discontinuous flux.

A wide range of numerical techniques have been developed in the literature for approximating conservation laws with discontinuous flux. While the list is extensive, we mention a few approaches: Godunov-type schemes
[
5
,
6
,
9
,
28
,
38
]
, relaxation schemes
[
34
]
, Enguist-Osher schemes
[
22
,
60
]
and upstream mobility schemes
[
47
]
. Other approaches include DFLU flux
[
8
]
, Roe-type schemes
[
66
]
, and Monte-Carlo methods for random conservation laws with discontinuous coefficients
[
14
]
. Furthermore, general monotone (A, B) entropy stable schemes and a local Lax-Friedrichs scheme were analyzed in
[
3
]
and
[
57
]
, respectively. The problem described in (
1.1
) has also been investigated from various other perspectives. Recent contributions include regularity results established in
[
27
]
, error estimates for monotone flux functions in
[
15
]
, flux stability under similar assumptions in
[
54
]
, compactness estimates in
[
39
]
, viscosity approximation in
[
35
]
, and BV regularity of adapted entropy solutions in
[
29
]
, among others. Despite these advancements, rigorous analytical results for second- and higher-order methods remain limited.

1.2
Scope and outline of the paper

Although first-order numerical methods are reliable and aid in ensuring the well-posedness of the underlying problems, second- and higher-order methods offer substantially improved accuracy, particularly for two and three-dimensional problems. Specifically, designing efficient second-order schemes achieves a desirable balance between the excessive diffusion of first-order schemes and the computational complexity of higher-order (third-order and above) schemes. Regarding second-order numerical schemes for scalar conservation laws with discontinuous flux, there has been some progress in the literature; see
[
7
,
20
,
56
]
. In particular, the convergence analysis of a class of second-order schemes to a weak solution was studied in
[
20
]
. This analysis was later extended in
[
7
]
to accommodate a broader class of numerical fluxes and to establish convergence to the (A, B)-entropy solution. Nonetheless, these studies rely on a non-local limiter algorithm to ensure the scheme is FTVD (flux total variation diminishing). While effective, the limiter algorithm introduces additional computational tasks compared to conventional second-order schemes. This naturally leads to a question: Is it possible to design a relatively simple scheme, such as one based on MUSCL-type spatial reconstruction, and establish its convergence to the entropy solution? To the best of our knowledge, this remains an open question, as also noted in
[
7
,
46
]
. In this article, we aim to address this problem by proposing and analyzing a comparatively simple second-order scheme, specifically, a variant of the Nessyahu-Tadmor central scheme (see
[
49
]
) that employs the minmod limiter for reconstruction.

It is worth noting that while upwind schemes provide higher-resolution solutions, they are often more restrictive due to the need for solving Riemann problems, either exactly or approximately, at mesh interfaces. In contrast, central schemes, such as the Lax-Friedrichs (LF) scheme, offer a significant advantage: they eliminate the need to solve Riemann problems, thereby reducing computational complexity and making them more attractive for certain applications. For problems of the type described by (
1.1
), a staggered Lax-Friedrichs central scheme was analyzed in
[
37
]
and more recently in
[
39
]
.
The second-order central scheme of Nessyahu and Tadmor, introduced in
[
49
]
, can be viewed as an extension of the first-order LF scheme to the case where the flux function is continuous or, equivalently, where the coefficient function
k(x)
in (
1.1
) is a constant. This scheme has been extensively studied further in the literature; see
[
12
,
45
,
50
,
52
,
53
]
. We also note that in
[
16
]
, a modification of the NT scheme, namely the Kurganov-Tadmor
[
41
]
scheme, was adapted to handle the discontinuous flux case arising in the modeling of continuous separation of polydisperse mixtures, and its superior performance over a first-order scheme was computationally illustrated. It is the purpose of this work to extend the Nessyahu-Tadmor (NT) scheme and provide a rigorous convergence analysis for problems of the type (
1.1
), involving a discontinuous coefficient
k(x)
.

A key highlight of this work is the application of the theory of compensated compactness to establish the convergence of the proposed second-order scheme. The compensated compactness framework serves as a powerful tool for proving convergence of numerical schemes, particularly in cases where bounded variation (
BV
) estimates are unavailable. It is well known that for conservation laws with discontinuous flux function, solutions may fail to possess bounded total variation; see
[
2
,
29
]
. A widely adopted approach that establishes the convergence of numerical schemes in such cases is the singular mapping technique, wherein the images of the approximate solutions under a suitable monotone map are shown to have diminishing total variation. This technique was introduced by Temple in
[
59
]
to prove the convergence of the Glimm scheme, specifically applied to a
2\times 2
resonant system of conservation laws for modeling oil displacement by water and polymer in reservoirs (also see
[
4
]
). However, applying the singular mapping technique to second-order schemes is challenging due to the difficulties in obtaining a time-continuity estimate in the absence of monotonicity of the scheme. This obstacle was overcome in
[
20
]
and
[
7
]
by imposing a FTVD limiter on the second-order scheme, which makes the scheme flux-TVD and consequently proving the time-continuity. Nevertheless, this limiting algorithm may seem somewhat tailored, lacking the straightforwardness of the standard slope-limiter methods.

In this article, we investigate the convergence of the proposed second-order central scheme using the compensated compactness theory, specifically within Tartar’s framework
[
25
,
58
]
. This approach was previously employed in
[
37
]
to establish the convergence of the Lax-Friedrichs scheme for scalar conservation laws with discontinuous coefficients; see
[
26
,
34
]
for further references. A novel component of our analysis involves deriving certain key estimates on the spatial differences of the approximate solutions. Furthermore, we derive a bound using the concept of one-sided Lipschitz stability studied in
[
52
]
, which plays a significant role in the derivation of the aforementioned estimates. These estimates collectively establish the
\mathrm{W}^{-1,2}_{\textrm{loc}}
compactness required in the compensated compactness framework. Finally, along with the derived maximum principle, the compensated compactness theory ensures the existence of a strongly convergent subsequence. Further, using a classical Lax-Wendroff-type argument, we can show that the limit of this subsequence is a weak solution to the problem (
1.1
).

To establish the convergence of the proposed scheme to the entropy solution, following the approach in
[
7
,
62
,
64
]
, we incorporate a mesh-dependent term into the slope limiter. The core strategy involves writing the time-stepping in the proposed second-order scheme in a predictor-corrector form, where the predictor step employs a first-order accurate Lax-Friedrichs time-stepping, and the correction terms guarantee second-order accuracy. In broad terms, the mesh-dependent term in the modified slope limiter ensures that these correction terms vanish as the mesh size approaches zero. Building on this idea, we show that, as the mesh size tends to zero, our second-order scheme converges to the limit of the first-order Lax-Friedrichs scheme, which was shown to be the entropy solution in
[
37
]
. We note that the analysis presented in
[
7
,
62
,
64
]
relies on bounded variation (BV) estimates of the approximate solutions. However, in the case of discontinuous flux, such BV estimates are not necessarily available; see
[
2
,
29
]
. In this context, a key novelty of our approach lies in utilizing a weaker estimate, which we derive, to establish the entropy convergence.

We have organized this article as follows. Section
2
provides preliminary details related to the problem (
1.1
). In Section
3
, we present the proposed second-order central scheme. The compensated compactness theory is outlined in Section
4
. A maximum principle is proven for the proposed scheme in Section
5
. Section
6
is dedicated to deriving a priori estimates. In Section
7
, using the compensated compactness framework, we prove the convergence of the proposed scheme along a subsequence to a weak solution.
Section
8
establishes the entropy convergence of the scheme, via the introduction of a mesh-size dependent term in the minmod slopes. Numerical results are presented in Section
9
and conclusions are drawn in Section
10
.

2
Preliminaries

2.1
Notations

We use the following notations throughout the paper:

\mathbb{R}_{+}:=[0,\infty),
denotes the set of non-negative real numbers. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R},
denote
\mathcal{I}(a,b):=(\min(a,b),\max(a,b))
and
\lVert\cdot\rVert:=\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathrm{L}^{\infty}}.
For sequences
\{v_{j}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
and
\{w_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
we denote
\Delta v_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}:=v_{j+1}-v_{j}
and
\Delta w_{j}:=w_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-w_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}},
respectively, for
{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
. Also for
a\in\mathbb{R},
denote
a_{+}=\max\{a,0\}
and
a_{-}=\min\{a,0\}.
For
a\in\mathbb{R},
the greatest integer function is denoted by
\lfloor a\rfloor
, and the sign function by
\mathrm{sgn}(a).
Finally,
\lVert\cdot\rVert_{BV}
denotes the total variation semi-norm.

2.2
Hypotheses

We assume throughout the paper that the initial data
u_{0}
is such that

\displaystyle u_{0}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R});\,u_{0}(x)\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}]\,\,\mbox{for}\,\,\mbox{a.e.}\,x\in\mathbb{R},

(2.1)

for some
\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}\leq\bar{u}.

Further, we impose the following assumptions on the coefficient
k
and the flux function
f.

(
H1
)

For some
\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}\in\mathbb{R}
such that
\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt}\leq\bar{k}
the function
k
satisfies

\displaystyle k\in(\mathrm{BV}\cap\mathrm{L}^{\infty})(\mathbb{R})\quad\mbox{and}\quad\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt}\leq k(x)\leq\bar{k}\,\,\mbox{for}\,\,\mbox{a.e.}\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}.

(2.2)

(
H2
)

For each fixed
k\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}],
the map
f(k,\cdot):u\mapsto f(k,u)\in C^{3}[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}]
and is strictly convex. Moreover, there exists
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\geq 0
such that
0<\gamma_{1}\leq f_{uu}(k,u)\leq\gamma_{2}
for all
u\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}].

(
H3
)

For each fixed
u\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}],
the map
f(\cdot,u):k\mapsto f(k,u)\in C^{2}[\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}]
with
\partial^{2}_{kk}f\equiv 0.

(
H4
)

The map
f_{u}:k\mapsto f_{u}(k,u)\in C^{1}[\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}].

(
H5
)

The flux
f
satisfies
f(k_{1},\bar{u})=f(k_{2},\bar{u})
and
f(k_{1},\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt})=f(k_{2},\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt})
for all
k_{1},k_{2}\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}].
For multiplicative flux
f(k,u)=kg(u),
this assumption reduces to
g(\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt})=g(\bar{u})=0.

(
H6
)

The coefficient
k
is piecewise
C^{1}
and is discontinuous only at finitely many points, say
D=\{x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{M}\}.

(
H7
)

Crossing condition: For any jump in the coefficient
k
with the corresponding left and right limits
k_{m}^{-}
and
k_{m}^{+}
respectively,

\displaystyle f(k_{m}^{+},u_{1})-f(k_{m}^{-},u_{1})<0<f(k_{m}^{+},u_{2})-f(k_{m}^{-},u_{2})\implies u_{1}<u_{2},

for any states
u_{1},u_{2}\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}].

Also, throughout this article we denote by
\kappa\in\mathbb{R_{+}}
a positive constant such that

\displaystyle\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\leq\kappa.

(2.3)

Remark 1
.

The analysis presented in this article is carried out under the assumption that the flux function satisfies the strict convexity condition stated in
(H2)
. However, we note that an analogous analysis can be performed for strictly concave fluxes as well.

2.3
Weak and entropy solutions

It is well established that the Cauchy problem (
1.1
) does not generally admit classical solutions, even when the coefficient
k
and the initial datum
u_{0}
are smooth. Instead, solutions to (
1.1
) are interpreted in the following weak sense.

Definition 2
.

(Weak solution) A function
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R_{+}})
is said to be a weak solution of (
1.1
) if it satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\left(u\phi_{t}+f(k(x),u)\phi_{x}\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{\mathbb{R}}u_{0}\phi(x,0)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x=0,

(2.4)

for all test functions
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}).

Weak solutions in the sense defined above need not be unique and an entropy condition needs to be specified to choose the relevant weak solution.

Definition 3
.

(Entropy solution) A function
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R_{+}})
is called an entropy solution of (
1.1
) if for all
c\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\int\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}(|u-c|\phi_{t}+\mathrm{sgn}(u-c)(f(k,u)-f(k,c))\phi_{x})\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t+\int_{\mathbb{R}}|u_{0}-c|\phi(x,0)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

(2.5)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\int\int_{(\mathbb{R}\setminus D)\times\mathbb{R}_{+}}|f(k(x),c)_{x}|\phi\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\sum_{m=1}^{M}\int_{0}^{\infty}|f(k^{+}_{m},c)-f(k^{-}_{m},c)|\phi(x_{m},t)\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t\geq 0,

for all non-negative test functions
\phi\in\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}),

where
D
is the set of discontinuities of
k
as given in
(H6)
.

Remark 4
.

The uniqueness of the entropy solution, as defined above, was established in
[
37
]
under the assumption of the crossing condition
(H7)
. However, in the two-flux case (
1.2
) with unimodal fluxes and a single flux crossing, this assumption can be omitted (see
[
22
]
). We note that our analysis can be extended to the setting of
[
22
]
also.

3
Second-order central scheme

The spatial domain is discretized using a uniform mesh of size
\Delta x
into intervals of the form
[x_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}},x_{j+\frac{1}{2}}]
where
x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-x_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}}=\Delta x.
For a fixed time step
\Delta t,
the time domain is discretized into points
t^{n}=n\Delta t
for
n\in\{0,1,\dots\}.
The ratio
\lambda=\frac{\Delta t}{\Delta x}
is kept as a constant throughout. Further, the initial data
u_{0}
is discretized as

\displaystyle u_{j}^{0}=\frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{x_{j-\frac{1}{2}}}^{x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}}u_{0}(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{for}\,\,j\in\mathbb{Z}.

Given the solution
\{u^{n}_{j}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
at the time-level
t^{n},
we compute the solutions
\{u^{n+1}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}},
at the next time level
t^{n+1}
on the staggered grid following the approach outlined in
[
49
]
.
We begin with a piecewise linear reconstruction of the cell averages
\{u_{j}^{n}\}
utilizing a minmod slope limiter, as a step towards achieving second-order accuracy in space. At the time-level
t^{n},
the solution in each cell
[x_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}},x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})
is then reconstructed as follows

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{j}^{n}(x)=u_{j}^{n}+\frac{(x-x_{j})}{\Delta x}\sigma_{j}^{n},\quad x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}\leq x\leq x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},

(3.1)

where the slopes are given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{j}^{n}=\mbox{ minmod}\left((u^{n}_{j+1}-u^{n}_{j}),\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j+1}-u^{n}_{j-1}),(u^{n}_{j}-u^{n}_{j-1})\right),

(3.2)

the minmod function is defined by

\displaystyle\textrm{minmod}(a_{1},\cdots,a_{m})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\mathrm{sgn}(a_{1})\min\limits_{1\leq k\leq m}\{\lvert a_{k}\rvert\}\hskip 15.6491pt\mbox{if}\ \mathrm{sgn}(a_{1})=\cdots=\mathrm{sgn}(a_{m})\\
0\hskip 98.16191pt\mbox{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Now, a finite volume integration in the domain
[x_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}},x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}]\times[t^{n},t^{n+1}]
yields

\displaystyle\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{j+1}}u(x,t^{n+1})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{j+1}}u(x,t^{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x-\int_{t^{n}}^{t^{n+1}}f(k_{j+1},u(x_{j+1},t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

(3.3)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\int_{t^{n}}^{t^{n+1}}f(k_{j},u(x_{j},t))\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\approx\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}}\tilde{u}^{n}_{j}(x,t^{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x+\int_{x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}}^{x_{{j+1}}}\tilde{u}^{n}_{{j+1}}(x,t^{n})\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-\Delta t\left(f(k_{j+1},u(x_{j+1},t^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}}))-f(k_{j},u(x_{j},t^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}}))\right),

using the midpoint quadrature rule in the time integration.
Applying the definition (
3.1
) in (
3.3
), we now write the staggered second-order scheme as

\displaystyle u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}=\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\frac{1}{8}(\sigma_{j+1}^{n}-\sigma_{j}^{n})-\lambda\left(f(k_{j+1},u^{n+1/2}_{j+1})-f(k_{j},u^{n+1/2}_{j})\right),

(3.4)

where the mid-time step values are computed as

\displaystyle u^{n+\frac{1}{2}}_{j}=u_{j}^{n}-\frac{\Delta t}{2\Delta x}f_{u}(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\sigma_{j}^{n},\quad{j\in\mathbb{Z}}.

(3.5)

Alternatively, we can also express the scheme (
3.4
) as

\displaystyle u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}=\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\lambda\left(g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\right),

(3.6)

where
g
is given by

\displaystyle g(k_{j},u^{n}_{j}):=f(k_{j},u_{j}^{n+\frac{1}{2}})+\frac{1}{8\lambda}\sigma_{j}^{n}.

(3.7)

For a fixed mesh size
\Delta x,
the piecewise constant approximate solution and the discretized discontinuous coefficient
k
are represented by the pair

\displaystyle

\displaystyle(u_{\Delta}(x,t),k_{\Delta}(x,t))

(3.8)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle:=\begin{cases}(u_{j}^{n},k_{j})\,\,\quad\quad\mbox{if}\,n\,\mbox{is even and}\,(x,t)\in[x_{j-\frac{1}{2}},x_{j+\frac{1}{2}})\times[t^{n},t^{n+1}),\\
(u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n},k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})\,\mbox{if}\,n\,\mbox{is odd and}\,\,(x,t)\in[x_{j},x_{j+1})\times[t^{n},t^{n+1}),\end{cases}

where
n\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
and

\displaystyle k_{j}:=\frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{x_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}}}^{x_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}}k(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x\quad\mbox{and}\quad k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}:=\frac{1}{\Delta x}\int_{x_{j}}^{x_{{j+1}}}k(x)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}x.

(3.9)

Remark 5
.

When the slopes
\sigma_{j}=0
for all
j,
the scheme reduces to the first-order Lax-Friedrichs scheme

\displaystyle u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}=\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\lambda\left(f(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\right),

(3.10)

considered in
[
37
]
. In other words, the scheme (
3.4
) is a second-order extension of the scheme (
3.10
).

4
Compensated compactness

The main ingredient of the compensated compactness framework is a theorem given below, the proof of which can be found in
[
37
]
. In the sequel, we will show that the proposed scheme (
3.4
) meets the requirements outlined in this theorem. This will ensure the existence of a convergent subsequence of approximate solutions generated from the scheme (
3.4
).

Theorem 6
(Compensated compactness theorem).

Assume that the hypotheses
(H1)
-
(H5)
hold true. Let
\{u^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
be a sequence of measurable functions defined on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}
that satisfies the following two conditions:

1.

There exist
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
with
a<b
, both independent of
\varepsilon
, such that

a\leq u^{\varepsilon}(x,t)\leq b\text{ for a.e. }(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}.

2.

The two sequences

\{S_{1}(u^{\varepsilon})_{t}+Q_{1}(k(x),u^{\varepsilon})_{x}\}_{\varepsilon>0}\quad\mbox{and}\quad\{S_{2}(k(x),u^{\varepsilon})_{t}+Q_{2}(k(x),u^{\varepsilon})_{x}\}_{\varepsilon>0},

belong to a compact subset of
\mathrm{W}^{-1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+})
, where

\displaystyle S_{1}(u)

\displaystyle:=u-c,\quad Q_{1}(k,u):=f(k,u)-f(k,c),

(4.1)

\displaystyle S_{2}(k,u)

\displaystyle:=f(k,u)-f(k,c)\quad\mbox{and}\quad Q_{2}(k,u):=\int_{c}^{u}(f_{u}(k,\xi))^{2}\,\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\xi,

for any
c\in\mathbb{R}
.

Then, there exists a subsequence of
\{u^{\varepsilon}\}_{\varepsilon>0}
that converges pointwise a.e. to a function
u\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}).

To establish the
\mathrm{W}^{-1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
compactness required by Theorem
6
, we will make use of the following interpolation result as well. For a proof of this result, see
[
24
]
.

Lemma 7
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
be a bounded open set. Let
q
and
r
be a pair of constants satisfying
1<q<2<r<\infty
. If
A
is compact subset of
\mathrm{W}^{-1,q}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega)
and
B
is a bounded subset of
\mathrm{W}^{-1,r}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega),
then

A\cap B\,\text{is compact in}\,\mathrm{W}^{-1,2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\Omega).

5
Maximum principle and
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-stability

This section establishes that the approximate solutions
u_{\Delta}
, as defined in (
3.8
), satisfy a global maximum principle, thereby yielding an
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-estimate as well.

Theorem 8
.

Let the initial datum
u_{0}\in\mathrm{L}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
with
\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}\leq u_{0}(x)\leq\bar{u},
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}.
Then, under the CFL condition

\displaystyle\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\leq\kappa\leq\frac{\sqrt{2}-1}{2},

(5.1)

and hypotheses
(H1)
-
(H5)
, the approximate solution
u_{\Delta}
(
3.8
) obtained from the scheme (
3.4
) satisfies the global maximum principle

\displaystyle\underline{\smash{u}}\leq u_{\Delta}(x,t)\leq\bar{u},

(5.2)

for all
(x,t)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{+}.

Consequently, the approximate solutions
u_{\Delta}
are
\mathrm{L}^{\infty}
-stable, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lVert u_{\Delta}\rVert\leq C_{u_{0}}:=\max\{\lvert\underline{\smash{u}}\rvert,\lvert\bar{u}\rvert\}.

(5.3)

Proof.

We use the principle of mathematical induction to prove this result. By the assumption on the initial datum
u_{0}
, the result holds for
n=0.
For
n\geq 0,
suppose
u_{j}^{n}\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}],
for all
{j\in\mathbb{Z}}.
We will now prove that
u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n+1}\in[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}]
for all
{j\in\mathbb{Z}}.

Adding and subtracting the term
f(k_{{j+1}},\bar{u}),
and using the hypothesis
(H5)
(i.e.,
f(k_{{j+1}},\bar{u})=f(k_{j},\bar{u})
), the difference
f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})
can be expressed as

\displaystyle f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})

\displaystyle=f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{{j+1}},\bar{u})+f(k_{j},\bar{u})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})

(5.4)

\displaystyle=f_{u}(k_{{j+1}},\tilde{\zeta}_{{j+1}})(u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}}-\bar{u})+f_{u}(k_{j},\tilde{\zeta}_{j})(\bar{u}-u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})

\displaystyle=f_{u}(k_{{j+1}},\tilde{\zeta}_{{j+1}})(u_{{j+1}}^{n}-\bar{u})+f_{u}(k_{j},\tilde{\zeta}_{j})(\bar{u}-u_{j}^{n})

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\lambda f_{u}(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{n})f_{u}(k_{{j+1}},\tilde{\zeta}_{{j+1}})\sigma_{{j+1}}^{n}

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\frac{1}{2}\lambda f_{u}(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})f_{u}(k_{j},\tilde{\zeta}_{j})\sigma_{j}^{n},

where
\tilde{\zeta}_{{j+1}}\in\mathcal{I}(u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}},\bar{u})
and
\tilde{\zeta}_{j}\in\mathcal{I}(u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}},\bar{u}).
From the expression (
5.4
), we get the estimate

\displaystyle\big\lvert f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})\big\rvert\leq\lVert f_{u}\rVert\left(2\bar{u}-u_{j}^{n}-u_{j+1}^{n}\right)+\lambda(\lVert f_{u}\rVert)^{2}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert.

(5.5)

From the CFL condition (
5.1
), we obtain
\displaystyle\frac{1}{4}+\kappa^{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}-\kappa.
This inequality, when combined with the estimate (
5.5
) applied on the scheme (
3.4
), and (
2.3
), yields

\displaystyle u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})+\frac{1}{4}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert+\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\left(2\bar{u}-u_{j}^{n}-u_{j+1}^{n}\right)

(5.6)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\lambda^{2}\lVert f_{u}\rVert^{2}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\kappa\right)(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})+\left(\frac{1}{4}+\kappa^{2}\right)\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert+2\kappa\bar{u}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\kappa\right)2\max\{u^{n}_{j},u^{n}_{j+1}\}+2\kappa\bar{u}=\left(1-2\kappa\right)\max\{u^{n}_{j},u^{n}_{j+1}\}+2\kappa\bar{u}\leq\bar{u}.

By the CFL condition (
5.1
),
2\kappa\in[0,1]
and hence the term
\left(1-2\kappa\right)\max\{u^{n}_{j},u^{n}_{j+1}\}\\
+2\kappa\bar{u}
is a convex combination of points in
[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}].
As a result, the last inequality in the previous equation holds true. Similar arguments as in (
5.4
), by adding and subtracting the term
f(k_{{j+1}},\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt})
in
f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}}),
gives another estimate

\displaystyle\big\lvert f(k_{{j+1}},u_{{j+1}}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})-f(k_{j},u_{j}^{{n+\frac{1}{2}}})\big\rvert\leq\lVert f_{u}\rVert\left(u_{j}^{n}+u_{j+1}^{n}-2\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}\right)+\lambda(\lVert f_{u}\rVert)^{2}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert,

which subsequently yields the lower bound

\displaystyle u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\frac{1}{4}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert-\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\left(u_{j}^{n}+u_{j+1}^{n}-2\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}\right)

\displaystyle{\hskip 14.22636pt}-\lambda^{2}\lVert f_{u}\rVert^{2}\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\kappa\right)(u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\left(\frac{1}{4}+\kappa^{2}\right)\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert+2\kappa\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}

(5.7)

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{1}{2}-\kappa\right)\left((u^{n}_{j}+u^{n}_{j+1})-\lvert u_{j+1}^{n}-u_{j}^{n}\rvert\right)+2\kappa\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}

\displaystyle=\left(1-2\kappa\right)\min\{u^{n}_{j},u^{n}_{j+1}\}+2\kappa\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}\geq\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt}.

The expressions (
5.6
) and (
5.7
) together yield the maximum principle (
5.2
).

6
A priori estimates

In this section, we derive certain essential a priori estimates on the approximate solutions obtained from the second-order scheme (
3.4
). These estimates form the foundation for the convergence analysis presented in Section
7
.

First, we focus on estimating the term
\sum_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}(u_{{j+1}}^{n}-u_{j}^{n})^{3}_{+}
, where
\{u_{j}^{n}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
are the solutions derived from the second-order scheme (
3.4
). The details of this estimate are outlined in the following lemma. The proof is lengthy and is provided in Appendix
A
.

Lemma 9
.

Assume
\{u^{n}_{j}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
is such that
\lvert u_{j}\rvert\leq C_{u_{0}},\,\forall{j\in\mathbb{Z}},
where
C_{u_{0}}
is as in (
5.3
). Let
\{u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
be obtained from
\{u^{n}_{j}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
by applying the time-update formula (
3.4
).
Under the CFL condition

\displaystyle\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\leq\kappa\leq\min\left\{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{7500\gamma_{2}},\frac{1}{4000}\right\},

(6.1)

the solution
\{u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n+1}\}_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}
satisfies the estimate

\displaystyle\sum_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}\left(\Delta u_{j}^{n+1}\right)_{+}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}(\Delta u_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}}^{n})_{+}^{2}-\frac{1}{500}\lambda\gamma_{1}\sum_{{j\in\mathbb{Z}}}(\Delta u_{{j-\frac{1}{2}}}^{n})_{+}^{3}+\Psi\lVert k\rVert_{BV},

(6.2)

for all
n\geq 0,
where

\displaystyle\Psi

\displaystyle:=72\lambda^{2}(C_{u_{0}})^{2}\lVert f_{uk}\rVert+114\lambda(C_{u_{0}})^{2}\lVert f_{ku}\rVert+\left(708(C_{u_{0}})^{2}+48\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\right)\lambda^{2}\lVert f_{u}\rVert\lVert f_{uk}\rVert

\displaystyle{\hskip 14.22636pt}+\left(48\lambda^{2}C_{u_{0}}\lVert k\rVert+132\lambda^{2}(C_{u_{0}})^{2}\gamma_{2}\lVert f_{u}\rVert+64\lambda\lVert f_{k}\rVert\lVert k\rVert+88C_{u_{0}}\right)\lambda\lVert f_{k}\rVert.

Next, we proceed to obtain a cubic estimate on the spatial differences of the approximate solutions.

Lemma 10
.

(Cubic estimate)
Let the initial datum
u_{0}\in(\mathrm{L}^{\infty}\cap\mathrm{BV})(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\lVert u_{0}\rVert\leq C_{u_{0}}.
For any fixed
T>0,X>0,
define
N:=\lfloor T/\Delta t\rfloor+1
and
J:=\lfloor X/\Delta x\rfloor+1.
Then under the CFL condition

\displaystyle\lambda\lVert f_{u}\rVert\leq\kappa\leq\min\left\{\frac{\gamma_{1}}{7500\gamma_{2}},\frac{1}{4000},\frac{7}{85+16C_{u_{0}}},\frac{\gamma_{1}}{\gamma_{2}\chi}\right\},

(6.3)

with
\chi:=228+13C_{u_{0}}+174C_{u_{0}}\gamma_{2}+12(C_{u_{0}})^{2}\gamma_{2},
the approximate solutions generated by the second-order scheme (
3.4
) satisfy the uniform bound

\displaystyle\Delta x\sum_{n=0}^{N-1}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\lvert j\rvert\leq J\\
j+\frac{n}{2}\in\mathbb{Z}\end{subarray}}\lvert\Delta u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}\rvert^{3}\leq C(X,T),

(6.4)

for a constant
C(X,T)
independent of
\Delta x.

Proof.

We need to define a linear function
\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}
which interpolates
g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})
and
g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})
as follows:

\displaystyle\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(u):=g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})+\frac{\Delta g_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}}{\Delta u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}}(u-u_{j}^{n})\,\,\mbox{for}\,\,u\in\mathcal{I}(u_{j}^{n},u_{j+1}^{n}),

where
\Delta g_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}:=g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n}).

Further, we consider functions
S,Q:[\hbox{\hskip 2.76042pt\hskip-2.76042pt\hbox{$k$}\hskip-2.76042pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.76042pt},\bar{k}]\times[\hbox{\hskip 2.86229pt\hskip-2.86229pt\hbox{$u$}\hskip-2.86229pt\hskip 0.0pt\raisebox{-1.0pt}{\hbox{\rule{3.44444pt}{0.32289pt}}}\hskip 0.0pt\hskip 2.86229pt},\bar{u}]\rightarrow\mathbb{R},
with the property that
\partial_{u}Q=\partial_{u}S\partial_{u}f.
Now, we define a quantity
E_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}
associated with the pair
(S,Q)
as

\displaystyle E_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}

\displaystyle:=S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})-\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)

(6.5)

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\lambda\left(Q(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-Q(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\right).

Our objective now is to reformulate
E_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}
in a suitable form, and use that to obtain the desired estimate. We begin with rewriting
E^{n}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}
by adding and subtracting suitable terms, as

\displaystyle E_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}

\displaystyle=S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})-\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)

(6.6)

\displaystyle{\hskip 14.22636pt}+\lambda\left(Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})\right)+R_{1}+R_{2},

where

\displaystyle R_{1}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)

(6.7)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-k_{j})+\mathcal{O}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-k_{j+1}),

\displaystyle R_{2}

\displaystyle:=\lambda\left[Q(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-Q(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})-\left(Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k_{j}-k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})+\mathcal{O}(k_{j+1}-k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}).

Now, we define parametrized functions for
s\in[0,1]
as

\displaystyle u(s)

\displaystyle:=su_{j}^{n}+(1-s)u_{j+1}^{n},

(6.8)

\displaystyle u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(s)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(u(s)+u_{j+1}^{n}\right)-\lambda(g(k_{j+1},u^{n}_{j+1})-\bar{g}(u(s))),

\displaystyle u(r,s)

\displaystyle:=ru(s)+(1-r)u_{j+1}^{n}\quad\mbox{and}

\displaystyle u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(r,s)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(u(s)+u(r,s)\right)-\lambda(\bar{g}(u(r,s))-\bar{g}(u(s))).

These functions satisfy the properties

\displaystyle u(0)

\displaystyle=u_{{j+1}}^{n},\quad u(1)=u_{j}^{n},\quad u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(0)=u_{{j+1}}^{n},\quad u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(1)=u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},

(6.9)

\displaystyle u(0,s)

\displaystyle=u_{{j+1}}^{n},\quad u(1,s):=u(s),\quad u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(0,s)=u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(s),\quad u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(1,s)=u(s).

Next, using (
6.8
), we write

\displaystyle

\displaystyle S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})-\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)

(6.10)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(s))\left(\frac{1}{2}+\lambda\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\prime}(u(s))\right)u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s-\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{1}S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Also, if
\tilde{Q}
is defined such that
\displaystyle\tilde{Q}_{u}=S_{u}\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\prime}=S_{u}\frac{\Delta g_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}}{\Delta u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}},
then adding and subtracting appropriate terms, we get

\displaystyle

\displaystyle\lambda\left(Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n}-Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n}))\right)

(6.11)

\displaystyle=\lambda(\tilde{Q}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-\tilde{Q}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n}))

\displaystyle+\lambda\left[\left(Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-\tilde{Q}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)-\left(Q(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})-\tilde{Q}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})\right)\right]

\displaystyle=-\lambda\int_{0}^{1}S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\bar{g}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(u(s))u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle-\lambda\int_{0}^{1}S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\left(f_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))-\bar{g}_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{\prime}(u(s))\right)u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Now, in view of (

) and (

), and rearranging the terms, we write

\displaystyle E_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}

\displaystyle=I+J+R_{1}^{n}+R_{2}^{n},

where

\displaystyle I

\displaystyle:=\int_{0}^{1}\left(S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(s))-S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\right)\left(\frac{1}{2}+\lambda\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\prime}(u(s))\right)u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s,

(6.13)

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\int_{0}^{1}S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\left(f_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))-\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\prime}(u(s))\right)u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Using integration by parts,
J
can be simplified as

\displaystyle J

\displaystyle=\bar{J}-\lambda\left[S_{u}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\left(f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))-\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(u(s))\right)\right]_{0}^{1},

(6.14)

where
\displaystyle\bar{J}:=\lambda\int_{0}^{1}S_{uu}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))\left(f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u(s))-\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(u(s))\right)u^{\prime}(s)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}s.

Further,

\displaystyle=\bar{J}-\lambda\left[S_{u}(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})(f(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})-g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n}))\right.

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle\left.-S_{u}(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})(f(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n}))\right]+\tilde{R}_{1}^{n}+\tilde{R}_{2}^{n},

where

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{1}^{n}

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\left[S_{u}(k_{j+\frac{1}{2}},u_{j}^{n})f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})-S_{u}(k_{j},u_{j})f(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\right]

(6.16)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\lambda\left[S_{u}(k_{j+\frac{1}{2}},u_{j+1}^{n})f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-S_{u}(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})f(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-k_{j})+\mathcal{O}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-k_{j+1}),

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{2}^{n}

\displaystyle:=-\lambda\left[\left(S_{u}(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})-S_{u}(k_{j+\frac{1}{2}},u_{j}^{n})\right)g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt+\lambda\left[\left(S_{u}(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-S_{u}(k_{j+\frac{1}{2}},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right]

\displaystyle=\mathcal{O}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}-k_{j})+\mathcal{O}(k_{j+1}-k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}).

At this stage, equating the right hand sides of (

) and (

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}

(6.17)

where we define

\displaystyle\bar{E}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}

\displaystyle:=S(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u^{n+1}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}})-\frac{1}{2}\left(S(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})+S(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right)

\displaystyle{\hskip 14.22636pt}+\lambda\left(G(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})-G(k_{j},u_{j}^{n}))\right),

Plugging in the derivative

\frac{\partial}{\partial r}u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}(r,s)=-s\Delta u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{n}\left(\frac{1}{2}-\lambda\bar{g}_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}^{\prime}(u(r,s))\right)

\displaystyle S(k,u)=\frac{u^{2}}{2},

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\lambda\Delta u_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}}\left[f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j}^{n})-g(k_{j},u_{j}^{n})+f(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},u_{j+1}^{n})-g(k_{j+1},u_{j+1}^{n})\right]

\displaystyle}{\displaystyle+\frac{\lambda}{12}(\Delta u_{j+\frac{1}{2}}^{n})^{3}f_{uu}(k_{{j+\frac{1}{2}}},\zeta_{1}),

Next, introducing the notations
